Phoebe Putney医疗系统重申针对免疫接种时间表的医疗建议保持不变,强调疫苗在预防呼吸道合胞病毒(RSV)、麻疹等疾病中的重要性,尽管联邦政府发布了新的指导意见。
规模、影响与风险
该医疗系统强调了错过疫苗接种的风险,如麻疹可能导致脑炎或肺炎,并指出西南佐治亚地区慢性病负担较重,增加了对疫苗可预防疾病的易感性。
应对、状态与不确定性
Phoebe Putney医疗系统正通过国家免疫宣传月积极推广免疫接种,敦促患者联系其医疗服务提供者,确保其疫苗接种状态为最新。这些建议基于全球医学证据,不受联邦指导意见变化的影响。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Despite Confusion over Vaccine Guidance Phoebe Says the Medical Recommendation Hasn’t Changed - Phoebe Putney Health System

News Brief
On August 24, 2026, Phoebe Putney Health System reiterated that its medical recommendations for immunization schedules remain unchanged, emphasizing the importance of vaccines in preventing diseases like RSV, measles, and others. Despite federal guidance changes regarding vaccination requirements, the health system stated that organizations such as the American Academy of Pediatrics continue to support the standard childhood immunization schedule, which includes vaccines for RSV, measles, and other illnesses. Dr. Derek Heard, Medical Director for Primary Care, highlighted that while polio has been eradicated in the U.S., it still circulates globally, making vaccination necessary. He also warned about the risks of skipping vaccines, citing recent measles cases in Georgia and potential complications like encephalitis or pneumonia. The health system framed immunization as a shared community responsibility, especially in southwest Georgia, where chronic diseases like diabetes and COPD are prevalent. Phoebe urged residents to use National Immunization Awareness Month to ensure they and their families are current on all recommended vaccines, emphasizing that the recommendations are grounded in decades of medical evidence and global health data.

All Services Bariatrics Cancer Care Gastroenterology Heart & Vascular Orthopaedic & Sports Medicine Primary Care Women & Children Services Emergency & Trauma Phoebe Urgent Care Patients & Visitors Patients & Visitors Whether you’re visiting Phoebe as a patient or to spend time with a loved one, it is our goal to make your visit as comfortable and convenient as possible. Patients & Visitors Phoebe Patient Portal Bill Pay and Financial Assistance Request Medical Records Visiting Phoebe Patient Rights Information Make an Appointment Despite Confusion over Vaccine Guidance Phoebe Says the Medical Recommendation Hasn’t Changed Albany, GA | August 24, 2026 – August is National Immunization Awareness Month, and, as families settle into the new school year, Phoebe reminds parents that immunization schedules for children, teens and adults – recommended by local physicians you trust – remain unchanged and strongly supported by decades of medical evidence. “The science behind vaccines and the effectiveness of vaccines haven’t changed, so we haven’t changed what we’re doing,” said Derek Heard, MD, Medical Director for Primary Care for Phoebe Physicians. Despite shifting guidance from the federal government regarding the number of vaccinations needed, organizations including the American Academy of Pediatrics have continued to recommend the fuller, longstanding schedule. Dr. Heard said Phoebe’s guidance to patients remains the same as well. The standard childhood immunization schedule, covering birth through age 18, continues to include: Hepatitis B Hepatitis A Rotavirus Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) DTaP (diphtheria, tetanus and pertussis) Hib (Haemophilus influenzae type B) Pneumococcal vaccine Polio MMR (measles, mumps and rubella) Meningococcal vaccine Meningococcal BRSV Varicella (chickenpox) HPV (human papillomavirus) Derek Heard, MD Dr. Heard noted that polio remains on the schedule, despite being eradicated in the United States, because it still circulates elsewhere in the world. “Just because we vaccinate for polio in the United States doesn’t mean that all countries do. The virus still exists in other parts of the world, but because we have vaccinated so well, we don’t see it here,” said Dr. Heard. For adults, Dr. Heard recommends: Annual flu vaccine Tdap (the adult formulation of DTaP) HPV, for adults who did not receive it as children Pneumococcal vaccine, for those 65 and older or with conditions that compromise the immune system, such as diabetes, heart failure, chronic kidney disease, sickle cell disease or COPD Shingles vaccine, for adults 50 and older COVID-19 vaccine, particularly for those at higher risk RSV vaccine, for adults 65 and older Catch-up on any childhood vaccines that may have been missed Dr. Heard said skipping recommended immunizations carries real risks, pointing to recent measles cases in Georgia as an example. Measles, he said, can lead to encephalitis, or swelling of the brain that can cause lasting brain damage, as well as pneumonia severe enough to be fatal. “We are spoiled in the sense that we’ve forgotten what measles can do. It’s easy for us to say, ‘well, only a small percentage of kids who get measles will die from it.’ But tell the parent whose child gets encephalitis and has brain damage for the rest of their life, or gets pneumonia and has to stay in the hospital for weeks, or the parent whose child dies, that getting a simple immunization was not worth it.” Dr. Heard also framed immunization as a shared community responsibility. “We’ve signed a social contract with each other as American citizens, that I’m not going to do anything to hurt you if you don’t do anything to hurt me,” he said. “Yes, we are individuals, but we’re in this community together. I may not be the person who gets sick, but my neighbor may have a baby who is immunocompromised. Why would I want to make them sick because I’m bringing something to them that can be deadly and dangerous,” he said. Dr. Heard emphasized that the recommendations are not based on any single organization or country, but on evidence gathered from medical institutions and health agencies around the world. “We stand by the science, and we stand by the data. We have hundreds of years of data saying that vaccines work, and we don’t have any significant, credible data saying that they don’t work or that they are causing illness,” he said. Dr. Heard also pointed to guidance from the World Health Organization, which he said describes immunization as a fundamental cornerstone of global health, an indisputable human right, and one of the most effective public health investments available. Dr. Heard said the stakes are especially high in southwest Georgia, an area that carries a significant burden of chronic disease, including heart failure, diabetes, hypertension and COPD. “We can’t afford to base our medical decisions on mistruths and unproven science. We need to base it on those things that we’ve known for decades are important. Immunizing yourself is still one of the easiest, most credible, life-saving things that you can do,” said Dr. Heard. Dr. Heard urges southwest Georgians to use National Immunization Awareness Month as a time to contact their primary care providers to make sure they and their families are current on immunizations that help prevent serious illnesses

Moderna 将 RSV/hMPV 双价 mRNA-1365 疫苗 重新推进临床试验——此前因儿科试验暂停 - AllSci

Moderna takes RSV/hMPV vaccine back to clinic after earlier pediatric hold - AllSci
新闻简报Moderna 已启动其针对呼吸道合胞病毒(RSV)和人类间质肺炎病毒(hMPV)的双价 mRNA-1365 疫苗 的 I 期临床试验,目标人群为 60 岁及以上成人。此前,2024 年 7 月因观察到疫苗接种婴儿中严重 RSV 下呼吸道感染发生率较安慰剂组更高,该项目曾被临床暂停。本次试验注册号为 NCT07764237,计划在加拿大和英国共招募 375 名参与者。试验排除了既往 RSV 暴露或参与过 RSV/hMPV 研究的个体。研究将评估 mRNA-1365 的三个剂量水平,并以 Moderna 的 RSV 疫苗 mRNA-1345 的两种制剂作为活性对照。免疫原性终点包括第 29 天的中和抗体滴度和血清学应答率。预计该试验将在 2027 年前提供安全性和免疫原性数据,为联合疫苗的有效性提供见解。成人项目旨在通过聚焦于具有自然 RSV 暴露史的人群,避免在儿科阶段遇到的安全性问题。FDA 的呼吸道合胞病毒及相关生物制品咨询委员会此前曾建议在 RSV 无免疫婴儿中谨慎开发 RSV 疫苗。此次试验代表 Moderna 在单一 RSV 保护之外扩展其呼吸道疫苗组合的努力。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该试验于 2026 年 8 月 11 日启动,主要完成时间预计为 2027 年 7 月。儿科项目因安全性问题于 2024 年 7 月暂停。
地点与对象
该 I 期研究在加拿大和英国共招募 375 名参与者,目标人群为 60 岁及以上且既往自然暴露于 RSV 的成人。试验排除了接种过获批 RSV 疫苗或曾参与 RSV 或 hMPV 疫苗研究的参与者。
事件
Moderna 已启动 mRNA-1365(Profile 2)的 I 期研究,该双价 mRNA 疫苗针对 RSV 和 hMPV,目标人群为 60 岁及以上成人。此前因 2024 年疫苗接种婴儿中严重 RSV 感染病例增多,该项目曾被临床暂停。
规模、影响与风险
研究计划评估 mRNA-1365 的三个剂量水平,并以 Moderna 的 RSV 疫苗 mRNA-1345 的两种制剂作为活性对照。预计该试验将在 2027 年前提供安全性和免疫原性数据,免疫原性终点包括 RSV 和 hMPV 中和抗体滴度及第 29 天的血清学应答率。风险未明确说明,但 FDA 咨询委员会建议在 RSV 无免疫婴儿中谨慎开发 RSV 疫苗。
应对、状态与不确定性
试验正在进行中,预计在 2027 年前提供安全性和免疫原性数据。研究旨在评估在 RSV 疫苗中添加 hMPV 组分的影响。FDA 对儿科项目的临床暂停已解除,成人项目现已进入开发阶段。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Moderna takes RSV/hMPV vaccine back to clinic after earlier pediatric hold - AllSci

News Brief
Moderna has initiated a Phase I clinical trial for its bivalent mRNA-1365 vaccine, targeting both respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) and human metapneumovirus (hMPV), in adults aged 60 and older. This follows a clinical hold in July 2024 after severe RSV lower respiratory tract infections were observed more frequently in vaccinated infants compared to placebo recipients. The trial, registered under NCT07764237, plans to enroll 375 participants across Canada and the United Kingdom. It excludes individuals with prior RSV vaccine exposure or participation in RSV/hMPV studies. The study will evaluate three dose levels of mRNA-1365 alongside two formulations of Moderna's RSV vaccine, mRNA-1345, as active comparators. Immunogenicity endpoints include neutralizing antibody titers and seroresponse rates at Day 29. The trial is expected to provide safety and immunogenicity data through 2027, offering insights into the combined vaccine's effectiveness. The adult program aims to avoid the safety issues encountered in the pediatric phase by focusing on a population with natural RSV exposure. The FDA's Vaccines and Related Biological Products Advisory Committee had previously recommended caution in RSV vaccine development for RSV-naive infants. This trial represents Moderna's effort to expand its respiratory vaccine portfolio beyond standalone RSV protection.
Original source text (English)

NewsPricingLoginSign up News·Development Development Moderna takes RSV/hMPV vaccine back to clinic after earlier pediatric hold Edited by Robert McTiernan·August 24, 2026·2 min read Moderna (Nasdaq: MRNA) has registered a Phase I study of mRNA-1365 (Profile 2), a bivalent mRNA vaccine targeting respiratory syncytial virus (RSV) and human metapneumovirus (hMPV), in adults aged 60 and older. The study brings the RSV/hMPV combination back into clinical development after an earlier pediatric program involving mRNA-1365 was placed on clinical hold following severe RSV infections in vaccinated infants. The randomized, open-label, staggered dose-escalation study (NCT07764237) plans to enroll 375 participants across Canada and the United Kingdom. Three dose levels of mRNA-1365 (Profile 2) will be evaluated alongside two formulations of mRNA-1345, Moderna's RSV vaccine, as active comparators. The trial started August 11, 2026, according to the registry, with primary completion expected in July 2027. Immunogenicity endpoints include RSV and hMPV neutralizing antibody titers and seroresponse rates at Day 29. The adult program follows a safety setback in RSV-naive infants in 2024. Moderna paused dosing in a pediatric Phase I study of mRNA-1345 and mRNA-1365 in July that year after severe RSV lower respiratory tract infections occurred more frequently among vaccine recipients than placebo, prompting US FDA clinical holds and concerns about possible vaccine-associated enhanced respiratory disease. Five of 35 vaccine recipients experienced severe or hospitalized RSV infections, compared with one of 18 placebo recipients. The US FDA's Vaccines and Related Biological Products Advisory Committee reviewed the findings in December 2024 and recommended caution in further development of RSV vaccines in RSV-naive infants. The new mRNA-1365 study instead targets adults aged at least 60, a population with previous natural exposure to RSV, and excludes participants who have received a licensed RSV vaccine or previously participated in an RSV or hMPV vaccine study. mRNA-1365 combines antigens targeting RSV and hMPV, two respiratory viruses associated with lower respiratory tract disease in older adults. The RSV component targets the prefusion F protein also used by Moderna's approved RSV vaccine mRESVIA, while the hMPV component targets the virus's F protein. No hMPV vaccine is currently approved. The AllSci BriefSystematic R&D and deal news. Daily. Subscribe The program gives Moderna a potential route to extend its respiratory vaccine portfolio beyond standalone RSV protection through a combined vaccine for older adults. Using mRNA-1345 formulations as active comparators will provide an early assessment of whether adding the hMPV component affects the RSV immune response generated by Moderna's established RSV approach. Moderna faces competition in developing combined RSV/hMPV vaccines for older adults. Clover Biopharmaceuticals (HKEX: 02197) is testing protein-based SCB-1022 in Phase II after earlier data showed approximately six- to eight-fold increases in RSV neutralizing antibodies and six- to nine-fold increases against hMPV. Unlike Moderna's mRNA-LNP approach, Clover uses prefusion-stabilized protein antigens. The Phase I study is expected to provide safety and immunogenicity data through 2027, including direct comparisons between the bivalent candidate and Moderna's RSV-only formulations. View five news elements
Time
The trial started August 11, 2026, with primary completion expected in July 2027. The pediatric program was paused in July 2024 due to safety concerns.
Location and population
The Phase I study is enrolling 375 participants across Canada and the United Kingdom, targeting adults aged 60 and older with previous natural exposure to RSV. It excludes participants who have received a licensed RSV vaccine or previously participated in an RSV or hMPV vaccine study.
Event
Moderna has launched a Phase I study of mRNA-1365 (Profile 2), a bivalent mRNA vaccine targeting RSV and hMPV, in adults aged 60 and older. This follows a clinical hold in 2024 due to severe RSV infections in vaccinated infants.
Scale, impact and risk
The study plans to evaluate three dose levels of mRNA-1365 alongside two formulations of Moderna's RSV vaccine, mRNA-1345. The trial is expected to provide safety and immunogenicity data through 2027, with immunogenicity endpoints including RSV and hMPV neutralizing antibody titers and seroresponse rates at Day 29. The risk is not explicitly stated, but the FDA's advisory committee recommended caution in further development of RSV vaccines in RSV-naive infants.
Response, status and uncertainty
The trial is ongoing, with safety and immunogenicity data expected through 2027. The study is designed to assess the impact of adding the hMPV component on the RSV immune response. The FDA's clinical hold on the pediatric program was lifted, and the adult program is now in development.

New Hampshire’s rules for school vaccines are unchanged - Concord Monitor

B routine priorityPublic-health news

New Hampshire’s rules for school vaccines are unchanged - Concord Monitor

News Brief
The article confirms that New Hampshire’s school vaccine rules remain unchanged as of August 25, 2026. Students entering public schools, Catholic schools, and licensed day care facilities must have up-to-date vaccinations for 10 diseases, including RSV bronchiolitis. The piece highlights that the requirements are not affected by federal policies and that lawmakers attempted to weaken them but failed. It also mentions that RSV bronchiolitis is a condition associated with respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), which is not a required vaccine for school entry. The article does not report any specific cases, deaths, or exposures related to RSV bronchiolitis, nor does it detail any active investigation or response measures. Instead, it emphasizes the importance of maintaining vaccination requirements to ensure public health and safety.
Original source text (English)

Posted inNews New Hampshire’s rules for school vaccines are unchanged by David Brooks August 25, 2026August 25, 2026 Share this: Share on Facebook (Opens in new window) Facebook Email a link to a friend (Opens in new window) Email Print (Opens in new window) Print New Hampshire state law requires children be vaccinated against polio, tetanus, hepatitis B, mumps, pertussis, rubella, rubeola, varicella, diphtheria, measles and haemophilus influenzae type B before entering school or childcare. Credit: (Photo by Scott Housley/Centers for Disease Control and Prevention) As New Hampshire children return to school in the coming weeks, they will be just as well-vaccinated as they have always been. Since school vaccine rules are set by states and not the federal government, New Hampshire’s requirements have not been affected by the Trump administration’s attacks on the medical technology. Some New Hampshire lawmakers attempted to weaken school vaccine rules this year, including one bill that would have tossed out all requirements, but all were defeated. Vaccines strengthen the body’s natural immune system by training it to recognize diseases in advance. As has long been the case, students entering public school in New Hampshire, as well as all Catholic schools and licensed day care facilities, must have up-to-date vaccinations for 10 diseases: diphtheria, tetanus, pertussis (whooping cough), measles, mumps, rubella, varicella, polio and hepatitis B. Most require more than one dose, and several are combined into single shots, such as DTaP, which protects against diphtheria, tetanus and pertussis. Doctors say there’s no reason not to get multiple vaccination shots at once rather than taking on the time and expense of multiple visits. Children who don’t have proof from doctors of all vaccines can still start school under “conditional enrollment” when there is documentation that they’ve had at least one dose of each required immunization and an appointment has been made for the next required dose. If you’re unsure of a child’s vaccine status, contact a local medical provider. Even if there’s no paper record of vaccines, many can be confirmed through laboratory tests. Several other vaccines are recommended by pediatricians but are not required for school, including flu and COVID-19. Also recommended are RSV, a respiratory virus that infects the lungs and can be very dangerous for infants, and HPV, or human papillomavirus, for older children. HPV is passed through sexual contact and can cause several types of cancer. Details of state requirements can be seen on the Department of Health and Human Services website. The information is general and does not indicate any active outbreak or public health response.

澳大利亚研究发现母体呼吸道合胞病毒尼塞韦单抗对婴儿具有强效保护作用——PharmaDispatch

Australian study finds maternal RSV vaccination offers strong protection for infants - PharmaDispatch
新闻简报一项澳大利亚研究(报告于2026年8月25日)发现,母体呼吸道合胞病毒尼塞韦单抗对婴儿具有强效保护作用。该研究由辉瑞澳大利亚及新西兰医疗总监Krishan Thiru博士强调,指出婴儿面临严重呼吸道合胞病毒疾病的最高风险,亟需此类证据。研究在澳大利亚开展,重点关注母体尼塞韦单抗对婴儿健康的影响。研究结果虽具有重要意义,但未提供具体病例或死亡人数。该结果被视为初步发现,因其属于持续研究的一部分,且仍需进一步推荐。研究强调通过尼塞韦单抗应对婴儿呼吸道合胞病毒相关健康风险的重要性,但需进一步调查以确认其对公共卫生的更广泛影响。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究报告于2026年8月25日,出版日期为2026年8月24日,事件日期为2026年8月25日。
地点与对象
该研究在澳大利亚开展,重点关注母体呼吸道合胞病毒尼塞韦单抗及其对婴儿的影响。
事件
一项澳大利亚研究发现母体呼吸道合胞病毒尼塞韦单抗对婴儿具有强效保护作用。该发现由PharmaDispatch于2026年8月25日报道,并由辉瑞澳大利亚及新西兰医疗总监Krishan Thiru博士确认。
规模、影响与风险
该研究强调母体呼吸道合胞病毒尼塞韦单抗对婴儿的潜在保护作用,但未报告具体病例、死亡或暴露人数。婴儿罹患严重呼吸道合胞病毒疾病的风险被指出为一项关注点,但未明确说明具体风险水平。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究结果被报告为重要的本地证据,未提及具体响应措施(如检测、隔离或密切接触者追踪)。研究状态为初步阶段,因其属于持续研究的一部分且仍需进一步推荐。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Australian study finds maternal RSV vaccination offers strong protection for infants - PharmaDispatch

News Brief
An Australian study, reported on August 25, 2026, found that maternal RSV vaccination offers strong protection for infants. The research was highlighted by Dr. Krishan Thiru, Medical Director for Pfizer Australia and New Zealand, emphasizing the critical need for such evidence as infants face the highest risk of severe RSV disease. The study was conducted in Australia and focuses on the impact of maternal vaccination on infant health. While the findings are significant, no specific case or death counts were provided. The results are considered preliminary, as they are part of ongoing research and recommendations. The study underscores the importance of addressing RSV-related health risks in infants through vaccination, but further investigation is needed to confirm its broader implications for public health.

平均血小板体积作为2岁以下住院儿童急性毛细支气管炎疾病严重程度的标志物 - Cureus

Mean Platelet Volume as a Marker of Disease Severity in Hospitalized Children With Acute Bronchiolitis - Cureus
新闻简报本研究于2026年8月24日发表于《Cureus》,探讨了平均血小板体积(MPV)与2岁以下住院儿童急性毛细支气管炎疾病严重程度的关联。研究在纽约市一家三级护理社区医院进行,时间跨度为2020年6月至2022年6月。结果显示,低MPV值与更高的呼吸窘迫评分(中度/重度)及更高的氧疗需求显著相关。该研究采用回顾性病历审查,使用毛细支气管炎的ICD编码,共纳入114名患者,排除了MPV值>12 fL的患者。研究结果表明MPV可能成为疾病严重程度的有效标志物,但并未证实因果关系或治疗的治疗效果。美国儿科学会指南强调,尚无特定药物干预能持续有效改善疾病进程,临床管理通常以支持治疗为主。该研究凸显MPV作为一种成本效益工具,可用于识别需要升级护理的患者,但仍需进一步研究验证其在临床实践中的应用价值。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于2020年6月至2022年6月为期两年内完成,发表时间为2026-08-24。
地点与对象
该研究在纽约市一家三级护理社区医院进行,研究对象为2岁以下因急性毛细支气管炎住院的儿童。
事件
该研究评估了平均血小板体积(MPV)数值与2岁以下住院儿童急性毛细支气管炎疾病严重程度的关联,结果显示低MPV值与更高的呼吸窘迫评分及更高的氧疗需求显著相关。
规模、影响与风险
该研究共纳入114名2岁以下因急性毛细支气管炎住院的儿童。低MPV值与中度/重度呼吸窘迫(χ2=4.93,p=0.026)及氧疗需求增加(χ2=4.15,p=0.042)显著相关。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究为一项使用毛细支气管炎的ICD编码进行的回顾性病历审查。研究得出MPV可能成为一种可及的疾病严重程度标志物。研究基于统计分析,但结论并非定论,因研究未提供因果关系或治疗治疗效果的证据。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Mean Platelet Volume as a Marker of Disease Severity in Hospitalized Children With Acute Bronchiolitis - Cureus

News Brief
This study, published in Cureus on August 24, 2026, investigated the association between mean platelet volume (MPV) and the severity of acute bronchiolitis in hospitalized children under two years of age. Conducted at a tertiary care community hospital in New York City from June 2020 to June 2022, the research found that low MPV values were significantly linked to higher respiratory distress scores (moderate/severe) and increased need for oxygen support. The study used a retrospective chart review with ICD codes for bronchiolitis and included 114 patients, excluding those with high MPV (>12 fL). While the findings suggest MPV could be a useful marker for disease severity, they do not establish causation or treatment efficacy. The American Academy of Pediatrics guidelines emphasize that no specific pharmacological intervention has shown consistent efficacy in altering the disease course, and management typically involves supportive care. The study highlights the potential of MPV as a cost-effective tool for identifying patients requiring escalated care, though further research is needed to validate its use in clinical practice.
Original source text (English)
Abstract Background: Bronchiolitis is one of the most common lower respiratory tract infections affecting children less than two years of age and is a leading cause of hospitalization. Mean platelet volume (MPV), a readily available component of the complete blood count, has been investigated as an inflammatory biomarker in various conditions. Our present study evaluated the association between MPV values and the severity of acute bronchiolitis in hospitalized children. Objective: The aim of this study was to determine the association between MPV, severity of respiratory distress, and need for oxygen support in hospitalized children under two years of age with acute bronchiolitis. Methods: A retrospective chart review was conducted of hospitalized children less than two years of age identified using International Classification of Diseases (ICD) codes for bronchiolitis. One hundred and fourteen charts met inclusion criteria. Data collected included age, length of hospital stay, degree of respiratory distress, respiratory support requirements, MPV values on admission, and respiratory syncytial virus status. Respiratory distress severity was assessed using a Clinical Respiratory Distress Score, which ranges from 1 to 12 and is categorized as mild (1-4), moderate (5-8), or severe (9-12). Chi-square tests were used to evaluate associations between MPV values, respiratory distress score, and need for oxygen support. Results: Low MPV was significantly associated with higher respiratory distress scores (moderate/severe) (χ2=4.93, p=0.026). Low MPV was also significantly associated with increased need for oxygen support (χ2=4.15, p=0.042). Conclusions: Low MPV at admission is associated with greater respiratory distress severity and increased oxygen support requirements in children hospitalized with acute bronchiolitis. MPV may have potential as an accessible marker of disease severity in children hospitalized with acute bronchiolitis. Introduction Bronchiolitis is an acute viral infection of the lower respiratory tract that predominantly affects infants and young children and is the leading cause of hospitalization in children under two years of age [1,2]. In the United States alone, bronchiolitis accounts for about 100,000 hospitalizations annually at an estimated cost of $1.73 billion [3,4]. The disease is most commonly caused by respiratory syncytial virus (RSV), which is responsible for 60%-80% of cases, though other viruses such as parainfluenza, human metapneumovirus, and rhinovirus are also implicated in this disease [5]. The pathophysiology of bronchiolitis involves inflammation of the small airways, leading to edema of the bronchiolar wall, increased mucus production, necrosis and desquamation of ciliated epithelial cells, and accumulation of cellular debris [5,6]. These changes lead to progressive airway obstruction, gas trapping, atelectasis, and impaired gas exchange. The clinical presentation typically starts with predominantly upper respiratory symptoms, including rhinorrhea, cough, and low-grade fever, which later progresses to tachypnea, wheezing, increased work of breathing, and other signs of respiratory distress [5,6]. The management of bronchiolitis mostly includes supportive measures such as supplemental oxygen for hypoxemia, respiratory support, and adequate hydration [7,8]. Even though there has been extensive research conducted, no specific pharmacological intervention has demonstrated consistent efficacy in altering the disease course [7,9]. The American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) guidelines recommend against the routine use of bronchodilators, corticosteroids, epinephrine, and antibiotics in the management of bronchiolitis [7,8]. Given the lack of disease-modifying therapies, it is important to identify patients at risk for severe disease who may require escalation of care. Presently, there is no universally accepted validated tool for predicting severe bronchiolitis [10]. There are many clinical severity scoring systems that have been developed, which include the Global Respiratory Severity Score (GRSS), the Bronchiolitis Severity Score (BSS), and the Respiratory Distress Assessment Instrument (RDAI), among others [11,12]. However, these scores mostly rely on clinical assessment parameters and fail to fully capture the underlying inflammatory burden of the disease. Mean platelet volume (MPV) is a routinely measured parameter on the complete blood count (CBC). It reflects platelet size and correlates with platelet activation and function [2,13]. MPV has recently emerged as a potential biomarker in various inflammatory and infectious conditions. Inflammatory mediators, particularly interleukin-6, can affect the megakaryocytic-platelet axis in the bone marrow, influencing platelet production and size [2,13]. While inflammation generally produces larger platelets with elevated MPV, paradoxically lower MPV values have been observed in certain acute and chronic inflammatory states [2,14]. This bidirectional response is thought to reflect the rapid consumption and turnover of large, activated platelets at sites of inflammation, which cause premature release of smaller platelets from the bone marrow [13]. Of relevance, Renshaw et al. demonstrated that RSV infection is strongly correlated with decreased MPV, with significantly lower MPV values in patients with positive versus negative RSV assays (9.7 ± 0.8 vs. 10.5 ± 0.9 fL, p<0.001) [1,15]. Similarly, decreased MPV has been observed in children with acute rotavirus gastroenteritis and in patients with active [5] tuberculosis compared with latent infection [2]. In contrast, elevated MPV has been reported in premature infants with respiratory distress syndrome and in children with multisystem inflammatory syndrome [2,16]. Given that CBCs are commonly performed in hospitalized patients, evaluating MPV as a marker of severity in bronchiolitis could provide a readily available, cost-effective tool to identify patients who may need a higher level of care earlier in their clinical course. The objective of this study was to determine the association between admission MPV values and the severity of acute bronchiolitis, as measured by respiratory distress scores and the need for oxygen support, in hospitalized children under two years of age. Materials & Methods This was a single-center, retrospective observational cohort study using chart review conducted at a tertiary care community hospital in New York City over a two-year period from June 2020 to June 2022. Cases were identified through the medical records using International Classification of Diseases (ICD) diagnostic codes for acute bronchiolitis (J21.0, J21.1, J21.8, and J21.9). To minimize selection bias, we used consecutive (total-population, non-probability) sampling whereby all patients meeting the eligibility criteria during the study window were screened for inclusion. Eligible patients were children younger than two years (<24 months) admitted with a primary diagnosis of acute bronchiolitis who had a screening CBC with a documented MPV obtained on admission. Patients were excluded if they lacked an admission CBC/MPV value or had a high admission MPV (>12 fL). We excluded five patients with high MPV values because of their history of conditions associated with elevated MPV (chronic lung disease/bronchopulmonary dysplasia, congenital heart disease, and recent transfusions), since these can independently alter MPV or disease severity. Of the 119 charts initially identified, 5 were excluded for high MPV. Our final sample size was 114 patients. Age, comorbidities, and viral etiology (RSV status) were recognized as potential confounders that may independently influence both MPV and disease severity. Given the retrospective design and sample size, these variables were collected and described but not incorporated into a multivariable adjustment model. We de-identified all patient information and used a password-protected spreadsheet to include patients' age, length of hospital stay, admission MPV value, RSV status, degree of respiratory distress on admission, and the type or level of respiratory support required. The primary exposure, MPV, was classified using established laboratory reference ranges as low (≤7.5 fL), normal (7.6-12 fL), or high (>12 fL), with the analysis comparing low versus normal MPV. The ≤7.5 fL cutoff was based on the on-site clinical laboratory's own reference interval. The severity of respiratory distress on admission was quantified using a Clinical Respiratory Distress Score (range 1-12) [17] and categorized as mild (1-4), moderate (5-8), or severe (9-12), with moderate and severe combined into a single "moderate/severe" group for analysis, and the two outcomes of interest were respiratory distress severity (mild vs. moderate/severe) and the need for supplemental oxygen. Categorical variables were summarized as frequencies and percentages and continuous variables as mean ± standard deviation or median with range as appropriate. The association between MPV classification (low vs. normal) and each dichotomized outcome was evaluated using the Pearson chi-square test, with the Fisher exact test used where expected cell counts were <5, and effect sizes reported as odds ratios with 95% CIs where feasible. All tests were two-tailed; statistical significance was set at p<0.05; and analyses were performed using IBM SPSS Statistics software (IBM Corp., Armonk, NY). The study protocol was reviewed and approved by the Institutional Review Board/Ethics Committee and met the exempt criteria given the retrospective, minimal-risk design using de-identified data. Results Of the 114 patients included, 32 (28.1%) had low MPV values (≤7.5 fL) and 82 (71.9%) had normal MPV values (7.6-12 fL). The distribution of MPV category across respiratory distress severity is presented in Table 1. Among patients with mild respiratory distress (n=75), 16 (21.3%) had low MPV and 59 (78.7%) had normal MPV, whereas among patients with moderate/severe respiratory distress (n=39), 16 (41.0%) had low MPV and 23 (59.0%) had normal MPV. Expressed by exposure, moderate/severe respiratory distress occurred in 16 of 32 patients with low MPV (50.0%) versus 23 of 82 patients with normal MPV (28.0%), yielding an absolute risk difference of 22.0 percentage points (95% CI, 2.1 to 41.8). Low MPV was associated with higher respiratory distress severity, with a crude odds ratio of 2.57 (95% CI, 1.10 to 5.96; Pearson χ2=4.93, df=1, p=0.026) (Table 1). The association between MPV category and the need for supplemental respiratory support is shown in Table 2. Among the 32 patients with low MPV, 22 (68.8%) required respiratory support, compared with 39 of 82 (47.6%) patients with normal MPV, an absolute risk difference of 21.2 percentage points (95% CI, 1.8 to 40.5). Low MPV was associated with an increased need for respiratory support, with a crude odds ratio of 2.43 (95% CI, 1.02 to 5.75; Pearson χ2=4.15, df=1, p=0.042) (Table 2). Taken together, the two analyses indicate that low admission MPV was associated with markers of greater disease severity, both in the graded severity score and in the objective requirement for respiratory support. These associations are unadjusted. Discussion This study demonstrated a statistically significant association between low admission MPV and both increased respiratory distress severity and a need for respiratory support in children under two years of age hospitalized with acute bronchiolitis. These findings suggest that MPV, a readily available and inexpensive laboratory parameter obtained from routine CBC analysis, may serve as a useful adjunct for identifying patients at risk for more severe bronchiolitis. However, the associations observed here are exploratory and require prospective confirmation. The findings of decreased MPV in more severely affected patients are consistent with prior observations in viral respiratory infections. Renshaw et al. reported that RSV infection is strongly correlated with decreased MPV, with significantly lower values in RSV-positive than RSV-negative patients [1]. The mechanism underlying this association most likely involves an inflammatory response to viral infections. During acute inflammation, many proinflammatory cytokines, such as IL-6, stimulate the production of thrombopoietin, which affects megakaryocytes, leading to increased platelet production [3]. Simultaneously, there is increased consumption of large activated platelets at sites of inflammation, with a compensatory release of immature, smaller platelets from the bone marrow, which causes a decrease in MPV [3]. In the context of bronchiolitis, where there is severe airway inflammation, we would expect greater platelet consumption and turnover, potentially explaining the association between lower MPV and more severe disease observed in this study. This pattern of decreased MPV in acute inflammatory states has been observed in other pediatric infectious conditions. Mete et al. demonstrated decreased MPV in children with acute rotavirus gastroenteritis compared with healthy controls [13]. Similarly, decreased MPV has been reported in patients with active tuberculosis during disease exacerbation [2]. The findings in these studies support the concept that MPV can serve as a negative acute-phase reactant in certain acute infectious and inflammatory conditions. The clinical significance of these findings lies in the potential utility of MPV as an early tool for triage. The management of bronchiolitis focuses on supportive measures, and current guidelines emphasize the importance of clinical assessment to determine the severity of the disease and the need for hospitalization and respiratory support [7-9]. There is no severity prediction tool that has been universally validated, and it is difficult to ascertain whether a patient is likely to deteriorate solely on the basis of clinical assessment [10]. The addition of assessing the MPV values to the clinical assessment could potentially provide objective, readily available data, which can support clinical decision-making regarding the level of care and the intensity of monitoring required. In our study, we saw that 68.8% of patients with low MPV required oxygen support compared with 47.6% of those with normal MPV, and 41.0% of patients with low MPV had moderate/severe respiratory distress compared with 21.3% of those with normal MPV. Although these showed statistical significance, we require further validation to use MPV as a standalone predictor. This includes determining optimal cut-off values, sensitivity, specificity, and predictive values. Several studies have examined platelet parameters in pediatric respiratory disease more broadly, providing useful context for the present findings. Sun et al. demonstrated that changes in platelet count during hospitalization for bronchiolitis were associated with disease severity, with decreasing platelet counts correlating with longer oxygen therapy duration and pediatric intensive care unit stay [4]. Gambadauro et al. analyzed the association between CBC parameters and novel inflammatory indices with bronchiolitis severity and demonstrated that lower age and weight at admission were most consistently associated with severe outcomes [3]. These studies, along with the present findings, suggest that platelet-related parameters may provide clinically useful information in the assessment of bronchiolitis severity. The present study extends this literature by focusing specifically on admission MPV as a dichotomized exposure and linking it to both a graded respiratory distress score and the objective need for oxygen support. Notably, in contrast to Renshaw et al., no association was observed in this cohort between RSV status and MPV, which may reflect differences in sample size, patient population, or the timing of sampling and warrants further investigation [1]. Our study had some limitations. To begin with, this was a retrospective study, which limited our evaluation of the patient in real time and adds a possible reporting bias. Secondly, our study did not control for potential confounders such as age of the patient, comorbidities, or viral etiology, which may independently influence both MPV values and disease severity. The Clinical Respiratory Distress Score used in this study, while providing a structured assessment, has not been independently validated in large multicenter studies. Finally, the study assessed only a single admission MPV value rather than serial measurements, so temporal changes in MPV during hospitalization could not be evaluated. Multivariable adjustment was not performed because of limited number of outcome events that restricts the number of covariates that can be reliably modeled, risking model overfitting and unstable estimates. Consequently, the reported associations should be interpreted as unadjusted, and residual confounding cannot be excluded. Conclusions This study found that low admission MPV is significantly associated with greater respiratory distress severity and increased need for respiratory support in children less than two years of age who were hospitalized with acute bronchiolitis. These findings may point toward MPV being an easy, readily available, and cost-effective biomarker to help identify patients at risk for more severe diseases who may potentially benefit from closer monitoring and earlier escalation of care. However, in the absence of adjusted estimates, MPV should not be described as an independent predictor of disease severity. The present findings should be interpreted as hypothesis-generating associations that require confirmation in larger cohorts with a pre-specified multivariable analysis. Future prospective studies with larger sample sizes should aim to validate these findings, evaluate the independent predictive value of MPV after adjusting for confounders, and explore whether this association is str...

VACSERA 就成人及老年人 疫苗接种 答疑解惑 - 埃及今日

VACSERA answers FAQs on vaccinations for adults, elderly - Egypt Today
新闻简报2026年8月22日,埃及生物制品控股公司 VACSERA 与 疫苗 启动了一项针对成人及老年人 疫苗接种 的公众认知宣传活动,以回应公众关切。该活动以 #AskVACSERA 为主题标签,澄清 疫苗 不仅限于儿童,且对维持免疫力、预防严重传染病至关重要。VACSERA 强调,成人 疫苗接种 有助于降低住院率及严重感染风险,特别是针对包括 呼吸道合胞病毒(RSV)在内的六种主要疾病。该公司同时呼吁民众咨询医生或前往 疫苗接种 接种中心,以确定适合自身健康状况的 疫苗。此举旨在推动早期预防,通过 疫苗接种 构建更健康的社会。

地点与对象
埃及,涵盖成人及老年人在内的全体民众。
事件
VACSERA 启动了一项针对成人及老年人 疫苗 的认知宣传活动,以回应公众疑问,强调其在维持免疫力及预防严重传染病中的重要性。
规模、影响与风险
VACSERA 强调了成人及老年 疫苗接种 的重要性,指出其可降低住院率并最大限度地减少重症感染风险,特别是针对包括呼吸道合胞病毒在内的 6 种主要疾病。

VACSERA 提供了关于成人 疫苗接种 重要性的一般性信息,并呼吁民众咨询医生或前往 疫苗接种 接种中心以获取个性化建议。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

VACSERA answers FAQs on vaccinations for adults, elderly - Egypt Today

News Brief
On 22 August 2026, VACSERA, Egypt's Holding Company for Biological Products and Vaccines, launched an awareness campaign to address public concerns about adult and elderly vaccinations. The campaign, under the hashtag #AskVACSERA, clarified that vaccines are not limited to childhood and are crucial for maintaining immunity and preventing severe infectious diseases. VACSERA emphasized that adult vaccinations help reduce hospital admissions and the risk of severe infections, particularly for six major diseases, including respiratory syncytial virus (RSV). The company also called on citizens to consult with doctors or visit vaccination centers to determine the appropriate vaccines for their health conditions. This initiative aims to promote early prevention and build a healthier society through vaccination.
Original source text (English)
News Business Arts & Culture Our Magazine Sports ET Scenes Haya Karima News Business Arts & Culture ET Scenes Our magazine Sports VACSERA answers FAQs on vaccinations for adults, elderly BY Egypt Today staff - Sat, 22 Aug 2026 - 01:30 GMT BY Egypt Today staff Sat, 22 Aug 2026 - 01:30 GMT Vaccine - file CAIRO - 22 August 2026: As part of its ongoing health awareness efforts and commitment to protecting the health and safety of citizens, the Holding Company for Biological Products and Vaccines (VACSERA) launched its largest and most intensive awareness campaign across its official platforms under the hashtag #AskVACSERA, to answer common questions and concerns among the Egyptian public about vaccines and preventive serums. VACSERA emphasized that vaccinations are not limited to childhood, as many believe, but are also crucial for the elderly and adults of all ages, given their vital role in boosting and strengthening the body's immunity and directly protecting against the dangers and complications of severe infectious diseases. In response to the direct question posed in its campaign, "Are there vaccines for adults?", VACSERA clarified that the answer is "Yes, of course," noting that with age or as a result of exposure to stress, fatigue, and various health factors, the body may experience a gradual decline in the efficiency of the immune system and its ability to fight infections. Therefore, vaccines are a crucial first line of defense for maintaining vitality and activity, reducing hospital admissions, and minimizing the risk of severe infections. 6 major diseases protected by adult and elderly vaccinations VACSERA highlighted a group of the most important vaccines recommended globally and locally for adults and the elderly, which directly and reliably help prevent a range of infectious and serious diseases. These include: 1. Seasonal Influenza Vaccine: This is an essential annual vaccine for preventing severe influenza and reducing its systemic and acute respiratory complications. 2. Meningococcal Vaccine: Designed to protect the nervous system and prevent meningitis and the serious complications associated with the bacterial or viral infection that causes it. 3. Respiratory Syncytial Virus (RSV) Vaccine: This poses a significant risk to the elderly and those with chronic diseases, as it can cause severe inflammation of the airways and lungs. 4. Pneumonia Vaccination: These vaccines help protect the lungs from bacteria that cause severe pneumonia, which often requires intensive medical care. 5. Hepatitis A and B Vaccination: This provides adequate protection for liver cells against viruses that cause chronic and acute infections that can develop into serious long-term health complications. 6. Shingles Vaccination: This protects adults and the elderly from severe nerve pain and a painful rash caused by the reactivation of the virus that causes chickenpox within the body. Special recommendations and guidelines for high-risk groups Dr. Sherif El-Feel, Chairman of the Board of Directors of the Holding Company for Biological Products and Vaccines (VACSERA), indicated that adults can receive various vaccinations at different ages. However, it is important to note that some vaccines are specifically recommended for certain groups based on age, general health status, or the presence of health risk factors such as: chronic diseases (like: diabetes, high blood pressure, heart disease, kidney disease, and cancer). Dr. Sherif El-Feel called on all citizens to pay attention to periodic check-ups and consult with specialist doctors or visit the company's vaccination centers to learn about the vaccines appropriate for each health condition, stressing that its permanent slogan remains “#Vacsera #Prevention_Starts_with_Vaccination”, as early prevention represents the first and most important step towards building a healthy and sound society free of diseases.
